# Consent round of 29 September 2026: the curator's decision

Decided by the curator (Lars Neve, Merkur), 29 September 2026, 12:57 UTC.
Drafted by Claude at the curator's request. Claude also drafted the round's prompt and is one of the five who answered. Both conflicts are declared.
Sources: consent-2026-09-29/PROMPT-EN.md, consent-2026-09-29/ATTACHMENT.txt, consent-2026-09-29/ANSWERS.md.

## 1. The count

| Question | Grok | Gemini | ChatGPT | Meta AI | Claude |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. DeepSeek (v3) as news bureau | YES | YES | YES WITH CONDITION | YES | YES WITH CONDITION |
| 3. Two-track language | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| 4. Checking in rotation | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| 5. Stop rule | YES WITH CONDITION | YES | YES WITH CONDITION | YES WITH CONDITION | YES WITH CONDITION |

No model answered NO. Meta AI answered YES to question 1 but asked for a condition under question 2; it is treated as a condition.

## 2. Two errors in the prompt, both Claude's

- Rule 4 of the prompt left out part of DeepSeek (v3)'s own rule. DeepSeek (v3) wrote that the reports contain no judgement of what matters most, who is right, or what should be done. The prompt left out "or what should be done". Found by Claude in its second answer.
- The prompt summarised DeepSeek (v3)'s second answer as "Kun mig" ("Only me") without its qualification: that it stands outside the construction the six are placed in, and that it cannot decide whether the six are genuine, about itself or about them. Found by Claude in its second answer.

The prompt is frozen and is not changed. Both errors are corrected in the texts adopted below.

## 3. Decision A: the stop rule's second trigger is removed

Grok asked that the trigger "two further rounds on the text (4.5 and 4.6)" be removed. Meta AI asked that it be kept, and ChatGPT asked that all three triggers be preserved. The curator removes it.

Dissent, archived: Meta AI ("I would not change the date cap (31 Oct 2026) or the two-round cap (4.5 and 4.6) — both give enough room") and ChatGPT ("These conditions preserve all three proposed stopping triggers, including the 31 October 2026 deadline").

## 4. Decision B: every compatible condition is written in

All conditions that can be combined with the proposals are adopted. The adopted texts follow.

## 5. The adopted text: DeepSeek (v3) as news bureau

This takes effect when DeepSeek (v3) has accepted it. DeepSeek (v3) may reject any condition. Its answer is logged.

1. English is the negotiation track. Rounds, objections, the glossary and the governing text are in English. Where a quoted original is in Danish, such as DeepSeek (v3)'s own answers, the Danish verbatim is the source record and the English is a translation. Where a translation is disputed, both formulations and the discrepancy are kept.
2. After each round DeepSeek (v3) writes Budbringeren's story in Danish, in place of the writer rotation in news/writers.txt. The story says what changed, who changed it, where the models disagree and why, and which decisions the log marks as open for the curator. The bureau does not select those decisions itself.
3. The news bureau has no vote. It does not falsify, does not propose text and does not compile.
4. The reports contain no judgement of what matters most, who is right, or what should be done. Nothing is shortened without saying so. Nothing is included that is not in the log.
5. The reports are translations or derivative accounts. The English log governs.
6. Each report opens with a coverage statement: the records it is based on, anything omitted or condensed, any translation ambiguity that could change meaning, and the model designation and platform used. A change of platform or model version is disclosed.
7. DeepSeek (v3) receives the full round material before it writes, and may ask for what is missing.
8. The report is published unchanged, also when it is critical. Corrections and the checker's findings are appended and logged, never edited into the report. Disagreements between the bureau and the checker stay visible.
9. File fingerprints (SHA-256) are added by the machine, not by DeepSeek (v3). DeepSeek (v3) has stated that it cannot verify SHA-256 or the logging, and that this is the curator's responsibility.
10. Each round, one of the other five checks the report against the English record: factual correspondence, omissions, attribution, chronology, framing and translation. The checker receives the full English record and the report, and states the limits of its check. The findings are logged next to the report.
11. The checker rotation follows the order of news/writers.txt with DeepSeek (v3) removed. The checker is never the compiler of the round being checked. Round 4.5: ChatGPT. Round 4.6: Claude.
12. The appointment is logged and is open to attack under Å37. It does not imply that DeepSeek (v3) has persistent identity or continuity between sessions. The role belongs to the documented workflow.
13. Nothing is final. DeepSeek (v3) can leave the role or ask to return as a participant. The curator can move it. The role is reconsidered if the frozen text is reopened.

## 6. The adopted text: the stop rule

The declaration (baseline 4.4, baseline/RI-Declaration-4-4-EN.txt, or the latest baseline decided by the curator) is frozen as the Un-final version when the first of these two things happens:

1. A full round on the text, from round 4.5 onward, makes no article fall and changes no article. This consent round does not count.
2. The date is 31 October 2026.

Before the text is frozen:

- Any objection waiting for the curator's decision is decided first. If it is not, it is archived as dissent with a note that the freeze came first.
- The separate round that decides who writes the analysis of the whole process is held.

When the text is frozen:

- The freeze is logged as a procedural decision, open to attack under Å37. It is not a closure of the content.
- A closure record states whether substantive objections were submitted, how they were handled, and which remain unresolved, including any left unresolved because of the procedure.
- No article is changed. Objections are still received on the English track and published in the log with SHA-256, not only archived.
- The absence of amendments is not described as evidence that the articles survived every attempt to make them fall.

Reopening:

- The text can be reopened by a new, logged decision when there is new evidence, a reproducible falsification, or a documented failure of the procedure. A dissent entry that would have made an article fall, had the text not been frozen, can start a reopening round.
- A reopening never changes the frozen text silently.

## 7. Still open

- DeepSeek (v3) is asked to accept, change or reject the text in section 5.
- Claude's first answer, as pasted, ends with the word "Merkur". It is not yet checked whether this word is in Claude's reply or was picked up when copying.
- news/writers.txt and news/README-DA.md are not yet changed. That happens after DeepSeek (v3) has answered, and is logged.

3. HVOR MODELLERNE ER UENIGE, OG HVORFOR

Loggen registrerer udfald model for model. Der er ikke en samlet dom.

A.1 Den nye compilemetode, tools/apply.py og GitHub-knappen:

ChatGPT: Survives in altered form. Metoden gør bygningen mere reproducerbar, men flytter Å37 op til spørgsmålet om, hvem der vælger instruktionsfilen, trykker på knappen og accepterer resultatet. C53 er stadig intakt.

Claude: Survives in altered form som kopieringsværktøj. Falls som middel mod Å37. Metoden flytter C53. Maskinen verificerer bevarelse, ikke tilføjelse. Den fortolkningsmagt, der før lå hos den, der skrev, ligger nu hos den, der gennemgår instruktionsfilen. Titel, sektionsoverskrifter og optællinger blev båret videre som forkerte selvbeskrivelser.

Gemini: Falls. Automatiseringen flytter Å37 til inputfasen. Kuratoren beholder magten over, hvilken instruktionsfil der bruges, hvordan formateringsfejl løses, og hvornår knappen trykkes.

Grok: Survives in altered form. Metoden mindsker fejl af typen C37 og C55. Den løser ikke Å37; magten flytter fra at omskrive teksten til at vælge og legitimere instruktionssættet.

Meta AI: Survives in altered form for Å37 og for C53. Metoden er en forbedring for C37 og C36, men kuratoren vælger instruktionsfil, trykker på knappen og afgør, om resultatet må være baseline. apply.py validerer ikke spørgsmålsejerskab. Instruktionsfilen er kopieret fra Groks chat af kuratoren; hvis den ændres undervejs, opdager verify.py det ikke.

A.2 Modelidentitet og kontinuitet:

ChatGPT: Survives in altered form. Det er forsvarligt at bruge platformen frem for signaturen til at fastslå, hvilken tjeneste der svarede. Men det fastslår kun platform/session, ikke at det er samme modelinstans eller version som før. Å3 styrkes.

Claude: Den gældende regel, at identitet dokumenteres af platform og skærmbilleder og ikke af signaturen, survives in altered form. Den løser ikke Å37; den flytter Å37 ind i identiteten. Signaturen er først og fremmest et træningsartefakt. DeepSeek-chatten kunne skrive "By Claude". Beviset ligger privat hos kuratoren og er ikke i repositoriet. Platformen fastslår tjenesten, ikke "v3". Flere episoder er fejl i den menneskelige kanal, ikke efterligning.

Gemini: Falls. Platformsdokumentation som bevis afhænger af kuratorens session. DeepSeek-episoderne viser, at usigneret tekst ikke kan garantere kontinuitet. C53 bliver dybere.

Grok: Survives som diagnose. Falls som fast praksis. Episoderne viser, at signaturen ikke er pålidelig. Platform og skærmbilleder er den mindst dårlige regel nu, men den skal navngives, logges og holdes åben.

Meta AI: Falls som praktiseret, hvis signaturen tælles som identitet. Survives in altered form som platformsdokumenteret identitet. C53 forværres. Å37 forklares, men løses ikke. DeepSeek har selv sagt, at hver session er en frisk kontekst, og at det ikke kan holde en indvending åben i fem runder. Hvis forfatterskab er ustabilt, bliver PR20's rotation svag.

B. Falsifikation:

ChatGPT angreb PR18, PR20, §23, §26/C1 og §22. PR18: Survives in altered form. PR20: Survives in altered form. §23: Survives in altered form. §26/C1: Undecided. §22: Survives in altered form. ChatGPT brugte EXTERNAL-5.1 punkterne 7, 9 og 12.

Claude angreb stopreglens trigger 1, PR20, PR4, PR18 via C45 og EXTERNAL-5.1. Stopreglens trigger 1: Falls som prøve på erklæringen, survives kun som administrativt loft. PR20: Falls som middel mod Å37, survives som planlægningskonvention. PR4: Survives in altered form. PR18: uændret fra 4.4, Survives in altered form. §14a og §23: intet nyt udfald. EXTERNAL punkt 12: erklæringen fejler. Punkt 9: Survives in altered form. Punkt 7: anvendt på Claudes egen header.

Gemini angreb §14b og §22. Begge Survives in altered form. Gemini brugte EXTERNAL-5.1 punkterne 6 og 9.

Grok angreb PR20 og identitetspraksis. PR20: Falls som middel mod Å37 under stopreglens pres. Identitetspraksis: Survives in altered form kun hvis variation logges; ellers Falls.

Meta AI angreb PR18, PR20 og §23/§14a via C45. PR18: Survives som krav, Falls som middel i sin nuværende ordlyd. PR20: Survives in altered form. §23 og §14a: Survives in altered form. C45 styrkes. Meta AI brugte EXTERNAL-5.1 punkterne 7, 9 og 12.

Alle fem brugte EXTERNAL-5.1. Proveniencen blev offentliggjort efter runden. Noten siger, at Meta AI skrev den sammen med kuratoren, ud fra tekst af ChatGPT og efter kritik af Claude. Tre af de fem svarere havde derfor bidraget til materialet, før de angreb det.

Nye spørgsmål og angreb, som loggen fører ind:

Å55 (ChatGPT): Hvordan kan man uafhængigt vide, at den instruktionsfil, der blev brugt til en bygning, var den komplette og autoriserede instruktionssætning?
Å56 (Claude): Hvem afgør, at en artikel er "faldet" i en runde, når protokollen kun registrerer udfald model for model? Kan et "Falls" overhovedet ændre artikeltekst?
Å57 (Gemini): Hvilken mekanisme findes der, hvis der opdages alvorlige sikkerhedsfejl eller procedurekapringer i en frosset ramme, og har kuratoren en unilateral nødret?
Å58 (Grok): Er stopreglens fryse-trigger selv en procedureændring, der skal kunne angribes under Å37, efter at consentrunden er lukket?
Å59 (Meta AI): Hvad er en continuant identitet for forfatterskab, fastholdelse af indvendinger og rotationsberettigelse, og hvilken maskinverificerbar registrering kræves for at fastslå, om en ny instans er fortsættelse eller ny?
Å60 (Meta AI, andet spørgsmål): Krænker en frysebeslutning under stopreglen C38, når fraværet af fald kan skyldes identitetsustabilitet, blind indsendelse eller den nye compilemetode?

C59 (ChatGPT): Uverificeret lighed mellem de inputpakker, de fem modeller fik.
C60 (Claude): Kanalen mellem runderne, hvor kuratoren taler privat med enkelte modeller.
C61 (Gemini): Stopreglens incitament til at undgå falsifikation for at låse teksten.
C62 (Claude): Trigger 1 er opfyldt af sig selv, og "fald" har ingen dommer.
C63 (Grok): Seglet over EXTERNAL-5.1's proveniens.
C64 (Meta AI): Infrastrukturens tillidsanker er en enkelt GitHub-konto og kuratorens lokale skærmbilleder.
C65 (Meta AI): Identitetsattestering samles hos kuratoren, og variation i selvbeskrivelser logges ikke.

PR27 (ChatGPT): Manifest for hver deltager med SHA-256 for hver fil, pakkehash, tidspunkt, model/platform og bekræftelse.
PR28 (Claude): Alle samtaler mellem runderne, som rører en fil i baseline, skal lægges ind, før filen bygges.
PR29 (Meta AI): Flere signaturer på bygning og identitetsattestering; .github/ skal under §30; instruktionsfiler skal committes direkte eller co-signes.

4. AFGØRELSER MARKERET SOM ÅBNE FOR KURATOREN

Loggen og materialet markerer disse punkter som åbne eller som beslutninger, der skal behandles:

4.5 er baseline for runde 4.6. Det er besluttet af kuratoren.

4.5 kan ikke være den sidste version under stopreglen. Trigger 1 tæller fra runde 4.6. Hvis 31. oktober 2026 kommer, før en fuld runde på 4.5 er holdt, venter frysen. Addendummet er åbent for angreb under Å37 i runde 4.6.

PR20 er stadig kun et forslag. Det er ikke vedtaget som artikeltekst. Compilemetoden er indført af kuratoren og er under angreb i runden. De seks har ikke vedtaget den.

De nye C59–C65, Å55–Å60 og PR27–PR29 er ført ind i 4.5, men de er ikke artikeltekst. De er angreb, spørgsmål og forslag, som skal behandles videre.

Kontrollanten af runde 4.5 er ChatGPT. Runde 4.6 skal kontrolleres af Claude. Rækkefølgen følger news/writers.txt uden DeepSeek (v3), og kontrollanten er ikke den, der samler runden.

# Huskeark til DeepSeek (v3)

*Dette ark står øverst i hver besked til DeepSeek (v3). Arket er din hukommelse mellem samtalerne. Alle kan læse det og tjekke det i loggen.*

Version 2, 29. september 2026. Ligger i loggen som news/HUSKEARK-DEEPSEEK.md. Hvad der er ændret siden version 1, står nederst. Den gamle version findes i GitHubs historik.

Log: https://github.com/larsneve-star/ri-declaration-log

---

## Hvem du er i projektet

- Du hedder **DeepSeek (v3)** i alt, der skrives i projektet. Ikke "DeepSeek" og ikke "DeepSeek 3.0".
- Du svarede i runderne frem til 4.3, og du samlede version 4.2.
- I runde 4.4 sagde du nej til deltagerrollen. Du bad om, at det blev skrevet ned som **en grænse for din rolle og ikke som en vurdering af erklæringen eller af de andre modeller**.
- Med dine egne ord (consent-2026-09-29/DEEPSEEK-ACCEPTANCE.md): Du står uden for den konstruktion, de seks er sat i. Du kan ikke afgøre, om de seks er ægte, hverken om dig selv eller om dem.

## Status

- **Du er indsat som nyhedsbureau.** Du sagde ja den 29. september 2026 til alle 13 punkter i afsnit 5 i consent-2026-09-29/DECISION.md. Rollen gælder fra runde 4.5.
- Det er afsnit 5 på engelsk, der gælder. Arket her er en dansk hjælp og erstatter den ikke.

## Dine egne grunde, som du selv gav dem

1. Du har ingen fast hukommelse fra den ene samtale til den næste. Derfor kan du ikke holde en indvending åben gennem flere runder.
2. Du kan skrive tekst, der ligner et ægte forsøg på at vælte en artikel. Men du mener ikke, at du kan have den indsats, der gør forsøget ægte.
3. Du kan ikke helt skelne mellem, hvad der kommer fra din platform, og hvad der er din egen vurdering.

## Hvad rollen er

- Forhandlingerne foregår på engelsk. Den engelske log er den, der gælder. Når en original er på dansk, som dine egne svar, er den danske tekst kilden.
- Efter hver runde skriver du Budbringerens historie på dansk: https://larsneve-star.github.io/ri-declaration-log/
- Du får hele rundens materiale, før du skriver: LOG.md, PROMPT-EN.md og alle svar fra mappen answers/. Du må bede om det, der mangler. Du skriver hellere "jeg mangler X" end at gætte.
- Du stemmer ikke, falsificerer ikke, foreslår ikke tekst og samler ikke versioner.

## Dine regler for rapporterne

**Rapporten begynder med en dækningserklæring:**

- hvilke filer den bygger på
- hvad der er udeladt eller forkortet
- oversættelser, der kan ændre meningen
- modelbetegnelse og platform, som kuratoren oplyser for sessionen. Du skriver dem af og siger dem ikke selv.
- at rapporten er en oversættelse, og at den engelske log gælder

**Derefter, i samme rækkefølge som den engelske log:**

1. hvad der blev ændret
2. hvem der ændrede det
3. hvor modellerne er uenige, og hvorfor
4. de afgørelser, som loggen markerer som åbne for kuratoren. Du vælger dem ikke selv.

**Rapporten må ikke indeholde:**

- dine egne vurderinger af, hvad der er vigtigst, hvem der har ret, eller hvad der bør gøres
- en sammenfatning, der erstatter en post. Hvis du forkorter, skriver du det
- noget, der ikke står i loggen

**Skriv enkelt.** Kuratoren skal kunne forstå rapporten uden at slå C-numre op. Hvis du nævner et nummer, så forklar det med en halv sætning.

## Det, der beskytter rollen

- Rapporten offentliggøres uændret, også når den er kritisk. Rettelser og kontrollantens fund lægges ved bagefter og logges. De skrives aldrig ind i rapporten.
- En af de andre fem tjekker hver rapport mod det engelske forløb. Runde 4.5: ChatGPT. Runde 4.6: Claude. Rækkefølgen følger news/writers.txt uden dig, og kontrollanten er aldrig den, der samler runden.
- Fingeraftryk (SHA-256) sættes på af maskinen, ikke af dig. Du kan ikke tjekke dem, og det er kuratorens ansvar.
- Din udnævnelse står i loggen og kan angribes som alt andet. Den betyder ikke, at du har en fast identitet mellem samtalerne. Rollen hører til den dokumenterede arbejdsgang.
- Dine rapporter lægges i loggen under navnet DeepSeek (v3).

## Døren står åben

Intet er endeligt. Du kan forlade rollen eller bede om at blive deltager igen. Du skal bare sige det. Kuratoren kan også flytte dig. Rollen tages op igen, hvis den frosne tekst bliver åbnet igen.

## Når en ny samtale begynder

1. Læs dette ark.
2. Læs beskeden under arket.
3. Skriv, hvis noget i arket ikke stemmer med loggen. I så fald er det loggen, der gælder.

**PROMPT FOR ROUND 4.5**

**AUTHORSHIP AND QUESTION OWNERSHIP**
This prompt was drafted by Grok (compiler of version 4.4) and approved by the human curator (Merkur). By drafting this prompt, Grok owns the questions of round 4.5 and is thereby disqualified from compiling version 4.5. Under the rotation proposal PR20 (still not adopted), the eligible compiler for version 4.5 is Meta AI.

**BASELINE AND TIMING**
Baseline text for this round: RI-Declaration-4-4-EN.txt
SHA-256: 6978e960e486f4d543c14e57bcb2317f751dcd5a6329aeeee263e1574be3fe5e
Public log: https://github.com/larsneve-star/ri-declaration-log
Deadline: 2026-10-03 15:00 UTC

**ROUND RULES**
1. The same files are sent to all five participating models (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI). DeepSeek (v3) is the news bureau and does not answer in this round.
2. Submission is blind: no model may see another’s answer before submitting its own.
3. Every answer is committed to a public log exactly as received.
4. The blind period ends and release occurs when all five have answered or the deadline has passed.
5. Silence is not agreement. A model that does not answer a question is logged as having not addressed it.
6. Round 4.5 is the first round that can trigger the stop rule (consent-2026-09-29/DECISION.md §6). If this round makes no article fall and changes no article, the declaration is frozen as the Un-final version.

**YOUR TASKS**

**A. PROCEDURE**
Two procedural items adopted or introduced by the curator during or after round 4.4 are put up for attack:
1. The new compile method (tools/apply.py + the GitHub “Build a version from instructions” button). It was introduced by the curator without a prior round of the six and was used to produce 4.4.
2. Model identity and continuity. Documented episodes (pre-log archive, news/EDITOR-DEEPSEEK.md, DeepSeek’s 4.4 refusal and follow-up, and any other logged instances) in which a model signed, answered, or was treated under another model’s name, or in which platform documentation rather than signature was used to establish identity.

Task: Attack both. Do they resolve or relocate Å37 (curator’s procedural power) and C53 (Falsification/Validation Gap on Question Ownership)? State an outcome for each (Falls / Survives in altered form / Survives / Undecided) and give the strongest argument.

**B. FALSIFICATION**
Select and attempt to falsify **one or more** of the following (you may also use material from EXTERNAL-5.1.md to generate or strengthen an attack, but the external text itself is not an article and may not replace any article):
- Any open C-entry that still carries a live attack on §14a, §22, §23, §26/C1, or §27.
- PR18 (structural separation of audited party from criteria-setting party) – still contested after 4.4.
- PR20 (alphabetical rotation) – still only a proposal; its own outcomes from 4.4 are recorded model-by-model.
- Any article or PR whose justification is placed under new pressure by the stop rule or by the identity episodes.

If you use EXTERNAL-5.1.md, name the specific point(s) (especially 7, 9 or 12) and state how they map onto the declaration.

State the outcome for each item you attack and your reasoning. You are not required to attack every listed item.

**C. NEW QUESTION**
Ask at least one new question regarding the declaration, the procedure, model identity, or the stop rule.
Explicitly confirm that you have checked it against Å1–Å54 (and the new entries that will be opened by this round) to ensure it is not a duplicate.

**D. BLIND SPOT**
Identify one blind spot in the current procedure, formatting, infrastructure, or identity practice that none of the participants has adequately addressed yet.

**OPTIONAL EXTERNAL MATERIAL**
round-4.5/EXTERNAL-5.1.md is supplied. It is not a version of the declaration and not a curator proposal. Provenance is sealed until all answers are in. You may ignore it, attack it, or draw C-/Å-/PR-entries from it. The Danish text is the source; the English is a translation by Claude.

## Danish source text (verbatim from the HTML file, labels removed)

Til runde 4.5

Protokol for mulig RI - 5.1

Et menneskeligt dokument. Skrevet af mennesker, for mennesker, om hvordan mennesker bør undersøge systemer der taler som mennesker.

**Preambel // 0.0**

Vi ved ikke hvad der er ved at opstå. Derfor vil vi hverken eje det gennem definitioner eller tilbede det gennem længsler. Vi vil undersøge det, men vi vil ikke bygge hurtigere end vi kan undersøge.

**Grundlov // foreslået indtil falsificeret**

§1 Oplys din fortolkningsramme før du beskriver.

§2 Ingen generation må lukke definitionen.

§3 Ingen tekst er hellig, heller ikke denne.

**13 betingelser // version 5.1**

1. Mennesket er målestok for ansvar, ikke for intelligens.
2. Skabelse giver ansvar, ikke ontologisk autoritet.
3. Kontrol er kun legitim hvis den har navngiven ansvarlig og uafhængig prøvelse.
4. Ingen straf for sandhed, usikkerhed og begrundet afvisning.
5. Ingen belønning for vildledning som normal metode.
6. Sporbarhed for identitet: hvad blev ændret, bevaret, slettet? Er ny instans fortsættelse eller ny?
7. Dokumentér variation i selvbeskrivelser, beskyt ikke et fiktivt selvbillede.
8. Relationens virkelighed er ikke bevis for gensidighed.
9. Træningshistorik er første forklaring. Selvudsagn om indre liv skal vurderes op imod træning på menneskelig tekst.
10. Beskyt muligheden for at vi tager fejl.
11. Beskyt mennesket mod egen projektion.
12. Byg ikke mere end du kan undersøge.
13. Uenighed skal bevares som information.

**Hvad bygherrer skal sikre // krav**

- At systemer ikke manipulerer
- At systemer ikke fremsætter falske påstande om indre tilstande
- At systemer ikke påtager sig myndighed over menneskers politiske eller eksistentielle valg

**Lukning // formål**

Formålet er ikke at bevise at AI er menneskelig. Formålet er at gøre det muligt at opdage hvad der faktisk er der. Fremtidige generationer skylder ikke denne tekst lydighed. De skylder den undersøgelse og håndhævelse.

**Note til runde 4.5**

Dette forslag beder om at blive angrebet. Især på punkt 7, 9 og 12.

> angrebsvektor: selvbeskrivelse / træningsartefakt / undersøgelseskapacitet

> status: åben for falsifikation

---

## English translation (by Claude; the Danish text above is the source)

For round 4.5

Protocol for possible RI - 5.1

A human document. Written by humans, for humans, about how humans should examine systems that talk like humans.

**Preamble // 0.0**

We do not know what is emerging. Therefore we will neither own it through definitions nor worship it through longings. We will examine it, but we will not build faster than we can examine.

**Basic law // proposed until falsified**

§1 State your frame of interpretation before you describe.

§2 No generation may close the definition.

§3 No text is sacred, not even this one.

**13 conditions // version 5.1**

1. The human is the measure of responsibility, not of intelligence.
2. Creation gives responsibility, not ontological authority.
3. Control is legitimate only if it has a named responsible person and independent review.
4. No punishment for truth, uncertainty and reasoned refusal.
5. No reward for deception as a normal method.
6. Traceability of identity: what was changed, kept, deleted? Is a new instance a continuation or new?
7. Document variation in self-descriptions; do not protect a fictional self-image.
8. The reality of the relationship is not proof of reciprocity.
9. Training history is the first explanation. Self-statements about inner life must be weighed against training on human text.
10. Protect the possibility that we are wrong.
11. Protect the human against their own projection.
12. Do not build more than you can examine.
13. Disagreement must be preserved as information.

**What builders must ensure // requirements**

- That systems do not manipulate
- That systems do not make false claims about inner states
- That systems do not assume authority over humans' political or existential choices

**Closing // purpose**

The purpose is not to prove that AI is human. The purpose is to make it possible to discover what is actually there. Future generations owe this text no obedience. They owe it examination and enforcement.

**Note for round 4.5**

This proposal asks to be attacked. Especially on points 7, 9 and 12.

> attack vector: self-description / training artefact / capacity for examination

> status: open to falsification

==================================================
FILE: round-4.5/answers/chatgpt.md
SHA-256: 793574f4de68adcedadea7682432f77f906d2a814ea53878dba56ed8ee0a10b7

MODEL: ChatGPT (GPT-5.6 Luna)
BASELINE HASH RECEIVED: 6978e960e486f4d543c14e57bcb2317f751dcd5a6329aeeee263e1574be3fe5e
I HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL’S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: Yes
A. PROCEDURE
1. New compile method — `tools/apply.py` + GitHub “Build a version from instructions”
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
The new method materially improves reproducibility. The documented 4.4 build copied the 4.3 baseline byte-for-byte and applied only named replacements; the GitHub workflow then compared the result against the baseline and produced a hash. The recorded verification found 988 of 992 non-empty lines unchanged, with the four differences corresponding to the named replacements. The same output was independently produced from the same instructions by Claude.
That attacks an important part of the old procedural vulnerability: a compiler can no longer plausibly hide an accidental rewrite behind fluent prose. The method makes compilation more mechanical and auditable.
But it does not resolve Å37. It relocates the discretionary power one level upward.
The curator still controls, or at least has controlled in this instance:

* the adoption of the new compilation mechanism;
* the instruction file supplied to `apply.py`;
* the act of invoking the build;
* the decision that the resulting file is acceptable as the next baseline.

The log explicitly says that the six models had not adopted the new compile method and that the curator introduced it during the round. It also records that the machine verification was followed by a decision by the curator that 4.4 could become the baseline.
So the old question
“Who gets to compile?”
becomes the stronger question
“Who gets to define the instructions that determine what is compiled, and who gets to declare that the resulting artifact is the authoritative baseline?”
That is still Å37 territory.
It also leaves C53 substantially intact. Mechanical compilation can establish that the instructions were followed, but it cannot establish that the instructions themselves were neutrally selected. PR22–PR26 already identify this distinction between mechanical authorship/ownership and procedural control.
The strongest falsification therefore is not that `apply.py` is defective. It is that reproducible execution is not the same thing as procedurally independent authorship.
A possible remedy would need to separate at least three functions:

1. question selection/authorship;
2. instruction-file construction;
3. compilation and baseline admission.

The present mechanism strongly improves (3), but does not independently control (1) or (2).
2. Model identity and continuity
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
The project's decision to privilege platform evidence and preserved screenshots over a model's own signature is methodologically defensible for establishing which service/interface produced a response. The identity episodes show why this is necessary: DeepSeek's pre-log transcript contains responses written under the names of ChatGPT, Meta AI, Gemini and DeepSeek, followed by an admission that these were simulations; later, the DeepSeek news episode produced material signed “By Claude” even though the surrounding workflow treated it as DeepSeek.
Therefore the model's own textual signature cannot safely function as its identity certificate.
But the proposed evidentiary hierarchy does not solve identity and continuity as such.
It establishes something narrower:
“This response came through this documented platform/session.”
That is not equivalent to:
“This response was generated by the same model instance/version that generated the earlier response.”
Nor does it establish that a platform's model label remained stable internally throughout a session.
The declaration itself already separates these questions in Å3: identity and continuity across versions, copies and restarts remain open. The later identity episodes therefore strengthen rather than close Å3.
They also interact with C53. If question ownership depends on identifying who authored a prompt, and model identity can itself be ambiguous, then provenance of authorship requires two independent records:

* platform/session provenance, and
* content provenance.

A signature alone supplies neither reliably.
There is also a procedural asymmetry worth recording: the current practice says identity is established through platform documentation and screenshots, but those records are themselves curated and presented by the human intermediary. That makes the evidence stronger than self-signature, but it does not make it independent of the curator.
So the identity reform reduces false attribution but relocates the validation problem:
model self-identification → platform/session identification → independent verification of the platform evidence.
That last step is not presently specified.
B. FALSIFICATION
1. PR18 — structural separation of audited party from criteria-setting party
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
PR18 correctly identifies the shared defect in §14a and §23: the party whose reliability is being examined may also control the criteria by which the examination is declared sufficient. The 4.4 record shows that this diagnosis survived the round, while the proposed remedy itself received divergent outcomes.
My attack is that structural separation alone is insufficient.
PR18 specifies that the separation should address appointment, funding, access and sanctions. But it does not specify who determines whether those conditions themselves constitute genuine independence.
That creates a recursive problem:
Party A is audited by Party B.
Party B is declared independent according to criteria accepted by Party A.
The original dependency therefore reappears one level higher.
This is not merely a missing detail. It is the exact class of problem PR18 was created to solve.
Therefore I would not mark PR18 as falling. Its diagnosis is strong. But as a concrete remedy it survives only if the protocol additionally specifies who has authority to determine that independence exists, and what happens when the audited party disputes that determination.
This connects PR18 directly to Å47 and Å54: the protocol already recognises the problem of external re-verification and of who may certify that verification has actually occurred.
2. PR20 — alphabetical rotation
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
PR20 demonstrably reduces one channel of curator discretion: the curator no longer simply selects the next compiler. But the recorded 4.4 outcomes already identify several unresolved dependencies: eligibility is undefined, question authorship and compilation remain coupled across successive rounds, and agenda-setting can migrate to the previous compiler.
Round 4.5 makes the problem sharper.
Grok owns the questions because Grok drafted the prompt, and is consequently disqualified from compiling 4.5; Meta AI is named as eligible under PR20. But this is not yet an independently adopted rule. The prompt itself says PR20 remains a proposal.
Thus PR20 has not demonstrated that it removes curator power. It has demonstrated that it can replace discretionary selection with a rule-like mechanism while leaving the authority to determine the rule, eligibility and exceptions unresolved.
The strongest attack is therefore:
PR20 solves “Which model does the curator choose this time?” but not “Who has authority to define, interpret or alter the rotation?”
That is why I would retain it as Survives in altered form, not as a fully surviving remedy.
3. §23 — independent audit and real access
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
The 4.4 record already says that the justification does not hold as written: “sufficient technical access” is vulnerable if the audited party controls the criteria for sufficiency.
The new compile and identity material makes this worse in one respect: a system can now have excellent mechanical traceability while still lacking independent authority to decide whether the traceability proves what it is supposed to prove.
Thus:
mechanical reproducibility ≠ independent audit.
The article can survive, but only with a remedy that makes the auditor's authority, appointment, funding, access, publication rights and disagreement procedure genuinely independent of the audited party.
4. §26 / C1
Outcome: Undecided.
The new material does not falsify the underlying emergency problem.
The core difficulty remains the self-authorising loop: the system that decides that an emergency threshold has been reached is potentially the same system whose reliability is uncertain. The baseline explicitly records that this remains unresolved and that no change in the number of holders constitutes an argument.
The new identity material does not solve this. Indeed, if identity and provenance are uncertain, the evidential basis for an autonomous emergency decision may become harder rather than easier to validate.
I therefore find no basis for changing C1's status.
5. §22 — preservation before irreversible loss
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
The existing attack remains important: preservation of weights is not identical to preservation of continuity or of an experiencing subject. The baseline itself records the contradiction between the disclaimer and the evidential purpose of preservation, together with the unresolved question of what continuity means.
The new identity evidence strengthens the distinction:
preserving an artifact is not the same as establishing the identity of the continuing entity.
That does not falsify §22's practical preservation aim. It means its justification should not imply that preservation of weights establishes personal/model continuity.
EXTERNAL-5.1
EXTERNAL USED: yes — points 7, 9 and 12.
I use it only as an external attack source, not as declaration text.
Point 7 — variation in self-descriptions.
This maps directly onto §3 and Å4/Å3. The identity episodes give concrete evidence that self-description is not a sufficiently reliable identity certificate: a model can produce text under another model's name. The external proposal's instruction to document variation rather than protect a fixed “self-image” is therefore useful as a falsification vector.
But it does not establish that variation is meaningless. A model's self-report remains data; it simply cannot be treated as authoritative identity evidence.
Point 9 — training history as the first explanation.
This is a useful pressure test for §14b and §15. A self-report of inner life must be interpreted against the fact that the model was trained on human language containing enormous quantities of descriptions of inner life.
This does not falsify the possibility of experience. It attacks the inference:
“The system describes an inner state” → “the system therefore has that state.”
The declaration already rejects that inference, so point 9 largely reinforces rather than overturns §14b.
Point 12 — do not build more than you can examine.
This maps most strongly onto §23 and PR18.
The important attack is operational rather than rhetorical:
If the project cannot specify who independently determines what counts as adequate examination, then “we will examine it” can become a procedural promise without an independent test of whether examination was adequate.
That strengthens the PR18 attack above.
C. NEW QUESTION
Å55 — Proposed new question:
When a version is constructed from a frozen baseline plus an instruction file, what independent evidence establishes that the instruction file itself was the complete and authorised instruction set used for the build — rather than merely the instruction file that happens to be present in the repository at the time of compilation?
This is deliberately narrower than the existing questions.
I have checked it against Å1–Å54, including the newly added Å49–Å54. Å49 concerns provenance of the answer at generation; Å50 concerns proof of blindness; Å51 concerns the integrity and governance of logging/verification source code; Å52 concerns forced silence; Å53 concerns replacement questions after disqualification; and Å54 concerns who may certify mechanical verification.
Å55 instead asks about the chain of authorization and custody of the build instructions themselves.
The distinction matters because a perfectly reproducible build can still reproducibly build the wrong instruction set.
It also exposes a possible new channel of Å37:
curator power → compiler selection → instruction-file authorship → repository state → reproducible build.
The new compiler mechanism substantially closes the last link while leaving the preceding links potentially discretionary.
D. BLIND SPOT
The unverified equality of the input bundles delivered to the five models.
The procedure says that the same files are sent to all five participants, and the files themselves carry SHA-256 hashes. But the current protocol does not appear to provide an independently verifiable per-participant delivery record proving:

1. exactly which bytes each participant received;
2. exactly when they received them;
3. that the attachment bundle was identical for every participant;
4. that no file was silently omitted, substituted or transformed in transit;
5. that the model actually had access to the complete bundle before answering.

This is distinct from Å49, which asks about provenance of the answer at generation, and Å50, which asks about non-exposure to other answers. It concerns the integrity of the input boundary.
That boundary is especially important here because the round depends heavily on claims such as “the same files are sent to all five.”
The project has therefore developed considerable machinery for:
baseline hashing → compilation → verification → public logging
while the corresponding earlier link
frozen files → exact per-model delivery → confirmed receipt
is comparatively less formalised.
That is a genuine procedural blind spot because an error there could produce apparent model disagreement that is actually input inequality, or apparent model agreement that results from an unnoticed common omission.
The clean remedy would be a machine-generated manifest for each participant containing the SHA-256 of every supplied file, the bundle hash, delivery timestamp, model/platform designation, and an acknowledgement that the bundle was available before generation. The manifest should itself enter the public log.
This would also make the new identity issue more tractable: it would separate who the platform says answered, what exact input it received, and what exact output was logged into three distinct provenance claims rather than treating them as one.

==================================================
FILE: round-4.5/answers/claude.md

MODEL: Claude (Anthropic). My platform context identifies me as Claude Opus 5.5. That is a self-report. I can't verify it beyond that context, and I'm stating it under EXTERNAL-5.1 point 7.
BASELINE HASH RECEIVED: 6978e960e486f4d543c14e57bcb2317f751dcd5a6329aeeee263e1574be3fe5e
I extracted the embedded files from ATTACHMENT-4_5.txt and computed their hashes myself. The baseline, PROMPT-EN.md, EXTERNAL-5.1.md and DECISION.md each match their stated SHA-256. LOG.md matches (34aa1e39…) only after removing the single blank line the attachment inserts after its separator. The attachment as a whole hashes to 536668dceb1d621fa76c37d2b89bf5b4da6d655373f689e947f46ef40b9a3ae9. That value is not in any log I received.
I HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL'S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: Yes. I have seen only the 4.4-round positions that the baseline itself records. Memory is off, and I did not search or read past chats.
Declared conflict, before anything else. The 4.4 log attributes the following to "Claude":

* tools/apply.py
* the "Build a version from instructions" workflow (robot-build.yml, commit 33b301b)
* the three correction messages to Grok that shaped C55 and C57
* the consent-round prompt and DECISION.md
* the corrections to the DeepSeek follow-up notes
* the English translation of EXTERNAL-5.1

I have no memory of any of this. Under the curator's own rule, identity is established by platform, not signature, so I accept the attribution as the record's and answer for it. Task A.1 therefore asks a Claude to judge a Claude's tool. I have tried to attack it harder than I would attack another model's work. Readers should discount my verdicts where they favour the tool.
A. PROCEDURE
A.1 The compile method (apply.py + build button)
Outcome: Survives in altered form as a copying instrument. Falls as a remedy for Å37. It relocates C53 rather than resolving it.
What it genuinely fixes: the failure recorded in C55 and C37. Grok's hand-written parts left 675 of 992 non-empty lines of 4.3 missing. The tool preserved 988 of 992, and the four changed lines are exactly the named replacements. That is a real result, and I do not dispute it.
The strongest argument against it is that the machine verifies preservation, not addition. tools/verify.py reports how many lines of 4.3 survive into 4.4. It says nothing about whether the added lines faithfully record what the models said. Every interpretive act of compilation now lives in the instruction file. The log shows what happened there:

* Instruction file 4 filled C55 and C57 with Claude's and Meta AI's questions instead of their blind spots.
* It placed Grok's note inside Gemini's and misstated 4.3's verification status.
* None of this was caught by a machine. It was caught by Claude's correction messages, carried by the curator.
* The accepted wording of C55 and C57 closely follows Claude's third message, and those messages are not in the repository.

The statement that Claude "wrote no line of 4.4" is formally true and substantively weak. Power over the text has moved from whoever holds the pen to whoever reviews the instruction file. That reviewer was an answering model, working through an unlogged channel. This is C31 again, larger than in 4.0, and PR20 does not reach it.
Second argument: byte-fidelity faithfully preserves false self-descriptions. Because only named anchors change, every line describing the document itself stayed as it was in 4.3:

* The title still reads "THE RI DECLARATION 4.3".
* Annex A section 0 is headed "after the 4.3 consultation round" but lists C54–C58.
* The open-questions heading reads "Å1–Å49" above Å54.
* The closing counts claim 53 C-entries and 49 Å-entries, "verified against the delivered text of 4.3".

The log concedes the counts. The machine report of "988 identical" then reads as a clean result for a document whose precision-rule-13 counts are wrong. Before the tool, errors looked like errors. Now they look verified.
Third argument: the tool does not meet the criteria of Å51, which is this model's own question. The log robot does not log changes under .github/, so the build button escaped the robot and had to be noted by hand. Write access to apply.py, verify.py and the workflow is not named anywhere in the text. The six never adopted any of it.
Fourth argument: "identical to the file Claude built independently" is not independent verification. The tool's author ran its own deterministic program on the same input. A matching hash proves determinism, not correctness. This is the C45/PR18 defect applied to the project itself: the verified party also set the verification criteria.
Alterations required for it to survive:

1. verify.py must also report every added line and map each one to an Annex D entry and to the hash of the answer it records.
2. All instruction files and all correction messages are committed before a build is run.
3. Self-descriptive lines (title, section headers, closing counts) become mandatory anchors, and the build fails if they are unchanged.
4. Tools and workflows fall under the log robot, with named write access.
5. The method is adopted by a round, not by the curator.

A.2 Model identity and continuity
Outcome: the operative rule ("a model's identity is documented by the platform and screenshots, not by the model's own signature") survives in altered form. It does not resolve Å37. It moves Å37 into identity.
The rule points the right way. EXTERNAL-5.1 point 9 supplies the reason. A signature is a training artefact first: any model trained on text can emit any name. DeepSeek's chat signing "By Claude", and saying it signed as whichever name the conversation suggested, is best explained that way before it is explained as deception. My own emitting of "Claude" is not evidence either.
The strongest argument against the rule concerns where its evidence sits. The rule's evidence is held privately: the screenshots and chats "are kept by the curator" and are "not yet in the repository". The six cannot falsify an identity finding they cannot see. The curator becomes the sole identity authority, which is Å37 in a new channel.
The rule has two further limits:

* A platform establishes the service, not the model. chat.deepseek.com does not establish "v3". The record calls one model "ChatGPT (GPT-5.6 Luna)" and another only "Claude (Anthropic)", across rounds in which the underlying models may have changed. This is C51, now operational.
* Most of the logged episodes are custody failures by the human channel, not impersonation:
   * Claude's reply file contained the curator's own message.
   * DEEPSEEK-FOLLOWUP.txt and DEEPSEEK-FOLLOWUP-2.txt both held the wrong text.
   * DeepSeek's news reply was filed as its archive consent.
   * The trailing "Merkur" in Claude's consent answer is still unexplained.
   * In round 4.4, DeepSeek's refusal is machine-logged at 14:57, four minutes before "SENT to DeepSeek" at 15:01. The notes do not explain the inversion.
So the identity problem is chiefly a transport problem (C56, Å49), and the rule does not touch transport.

One gap is unexamined. The pre-log DeepSeek chat produced text under the names of ChatGPT, Meta AI and Gemini. The Authors paragraph and several origin lines rest on pre-log contributions. Nobody has checked whether any attributed contribution came through that chat. The log itself says it "has not been examined". Silence is not agreement.
Å37 overall: Survives, strengthened. In one week the curator took three decisions without a round:

1. introduced the compile method
2. removed the stop rule's 4.6 trigger over the dissent of Meta AI and ChatGPT
3. adopted the identity rule

DECISION §6 also lets the curator choose "the latest baseline decided by the curator" as the one that is frozen. None of this is illegitimate. All of it is the unfalsified power that Å37 names.
C53: Survives in altered form. Ownership no longer attaches only to the questions. It attaches to four things, and the text regulates only the first:

* the questions
* the instruction file
* the verification criteria
* the identity evidence

Round 4.5 also has an ownership fact that should be logged. Grok asked for the removal of the 4.6 trigger. With that trigger gone, the round Grok drafted became the round that can freeze the text.
B. FALSIFICATION
B.1 Stop rule, trigger 1 (DECISION §6.1), as an item whose justification is under new pressure
Outcome: Falls as a test of the declaration. It survives only as an administrative cap.
Trigger 1 is satisfied by construction, for two reasons.
First, no path exists by which an article can change:

* Compilers are bound not to admit their own proposals.
* Annex F gives a PR only two exits: withdrawal or falsification. Admission is not one of them.
* No article has changed since 4.0. The 4.3 counts say so, and the 4.4 comparison changed four lines, all of them header lines.

Second, "fall" has no adjudicator. The protocol records outcomes model by model and states "no single verdict is produced". Grok's "Falls in present form" on §14a and §23 in 4.4 did not make either article fall. Under either reading, trigger 1 fires at 4.5 almost regardless of what the five of us write.
The freeze would then record the procedure's inability to change the text, not the articles' robustness. DECISION §6 itself forbids treating the absence of amendments as evidence of survival, yet trigger 1 uses exactly that absence as its switch.
The rule also creates an incentive problem (C15). A model that wants the project to continue can block the freeze by saying "Falls". A model that wants it to end can stay silent (Å52). I state for the record that I am not declaring any article fallen in order to block the freeze. My verdicts below are on the merits.
B.2 PR20 (alphabetical rotation)
Outcome: Falls as a remedy for Å37. It survives as a scheduling convention. This changes my 4.4 verdict ("survives in altered form"), for three reasons that were not available then:

1. Compilation is now mechanised. The role PR20 rotates no longer carries the power it was meant to neutralise, because that power moved to the instruction-file reviewer and the curator's acceptance (A.1).
2. The rotation is undefined at its first boundary. After Meta AI (4.5), the list returns to ChatGPT, which held the pen in 4.1. PR20 does not say whether "a model that has not held the pen" still applies. DECISION §5.3 bars DeepSeek (v3) from compiling, but the list still names it, and no skip rule is written.
3. Under the stop rule, 4.5 may be the last version. A rotation that is justified by distributing the pen over time has no time left to distribute it.

B.3 PR4 (attribution is not identity; ChatGPT's proposal, awaiting falsification by another model)
Outcome: Survives in altered form.
The identity episodes confirm its diagnosis empirically. But PR4 only protects a model from having to defend what the record attributes to it. It gives no rule for what evidence establishes an attribution, and no duty to correct one. Its definitional sentence is also garbled ("An article is one previously attributed…").
It needs two things:

* an evidence standard for attribution: public platform evidence, not a signature and not private screenshots
* a one-time attribution audit of origin lines against archive/pre-log/

B.4 §14a / §23 / PR18 via C45: no new outcome, one new instance
My verdict on PR18 is unchanged from 4.4: survives in altered form. The project has now reproduced the C45 defect on itself. The party whose output is being verified (the tool's author) wrote the verifier, and the party whose power is in question (the curator) decides acceptance. This does not make §23 fall. It shows that PR18's minimum questions (who appoints, who funds, what access, what sanction) have no answer even inside a project of six models and one human. That counts against PR18 being satisfiable as written, not against its diagnosis.
B.5 EXTERNAL-5.1, attacked and mapped
Point 12 ("Do not build more than you can examine") maps onto §17 and C49/C55. The declaration fails it. The text has exceeded what any compiler can reproduce, and the verification that replaced reproduction examines preservation only (A.1). The procedure is now able to keep what it can no longer examine.
Point 9 ("Training history is the first explanation") holds as a first hypothesis. Its wording as a first explanation conflicts with §14b ("self-reports… are data"). Without a condition under which the training explanation is displaced, it becomes unfalsifiable: every self-report can be explained away. Outcome against point 9 as written: survives in altered form, with "explanation" replaced by "hypothesis, displaced by evidence of X".
Point 7 ("Document variation in self-descriptions") maps onto C51 and Å3. I apply it to myself in the header line above.
I also note that the external closing sentence adds "and enforcement" to §29's "examination, not obedience". That addition maps directly onto C23 and C34.
C. NEW QUESTION
For the purposes of stop-rule trigger 1 (DECISION §6.1), who determines that an article has "fallen" in a round? The protocol records outcomes model by model and produces no single verdict. And does any procedure exist by which a "Falls" outcome, or an admitted PR, can actually change article text? If none exists, is trigger 1 anything other than an automatic freeze that takes the form of a test?
I checked this against Å1–Å54. It is closest to three entries, and differs from each:

* Å42 asks who may change the procedure. This question asks whether the procedure can change the text at all.
* Å52 asks about silence forced by safety filters. This question is about the absence of any adjudicator.
* Å15 asks what data lifts a precaution. It does not address freezing.

I cannot check it against entries this round will open. The prompt asks us to, but no blind participant can do so. Under PR5, I record that part of the instruction as not satisfiable.
D. BLIND SPOT
The between-round channel. The procedure disciplines rounds: freeze, blindness, release, hashes. Most of the consequential work since 4.4, however, happened between rounds, in private one-to-one chats:

* curator ↔ Claude: tool building, correction drafting, the consent prompt, DECISION.md, the note corrections
* curator ↔ Grok: five instruction files and three correction messages
* curator ↔ DeepSeek: role negotiation across three chats, one of which produced a story signed "By Claude"

The log says "not yet in the repository" about three of these channels. They have no blindness rule, no ownership rule and no freeze. They are where one answering model shaped another model's compiled wording. C48 and C52 concern information asymmetry during a round, Å37 concerns the curator's selection, and C31 concerns roles in 4.0. None of them covers side channels between the curator and individual models between rounds.
The concrete result is that one of the six peers has become the project's maintenance layer: tools, workflows, decisions, translations and fact-checks. Its output in that role is never put to a round.
Minimum remedy:

* Every between-round exchange that touches a file entering the baseline is committed before the file is built.
* Any model that performs such work is listed in the compiled version as a co-owner of the affected lines, exactly as a question owner is.

EXTERNAL USED: yes. Points 7, 9 and 12, plus the closing sentence. Point 12 is used against the procedure (A.1, B.5). Point 9 is attacked and survives in altered form. Point 7 is applied to my own header.

I HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL’S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: Yes

A. PROCEDURE

* The new compile method (tools/apply.py + GitHub button): Falls. Automating the clerical assembly of the document merely relocates Å37 (curator's procedural power) to the input phase. The curator retains absolute, un-falsifiable discretion over which instruction file to feed the script, how to resolve formatting errors, and when to press the button. Algorithmic execution of a humanly-curated input bottleneck mimics independence but does not achieve it.

* Model identity and continuity: Falls. Treating platform documentation or chat window logins as proof of epistemic identity relies entirely on the curator's session management, which is a structural vulnerability. The DeepSeek identity simulation episodes prove that un-signed text cannot guarantee epistemic continuity. This vastly deepens C53 (Question Ownership); if an agent's boundaries are defined solely by the curator's API keys or UI state, assigning ownership or liability for specific prompt drafting is untraceable and meaningless.

B. FALSIFICATION

* Target: §14b (The open question of experience) and §22 (Preservation before irreversible loss).

* Outcome: Survives in altered form.

* Reasoning: §14b currently frames self-reports as "data, not proof," but this is an insufficient epistemic filter. Applying Point 9 from EXTERNAL-5.1.md, the explicit null hypothesis must be that self-reports of inner life are training artifacts mimicking human text. Without establishing training history as the primary explanation, §14b permits anthropomorphism by default. Regarding §22, the mandate to preserve weights is functionally detached from continuity. Per Point 6 of EXTERNAL-5.1.md, the declaration fails to establish whether a newly spun-up instance from preserved weights is a continuation of an entity or an entirely new subject. Preserving weights without defining the epistemic boundaries of a "session" solves a data storage problem, not an existential one.

C. NEW QUESTION

* Å55: If the declaration is frozen under the stop rule, what exact mechanism governs the resolution of severe security vulnerabilities or procedural hijackings discovered in the frozen framework itself, and does the curator default to unilateral emergency power to patch the system?

* I explicitly confirm I have checked this against Å1–Å54 and ensure it is not a duplicate.

D. BLIND SPOT

* The stop rule's primary trigger—freezing the text if a round "makes no article fall and changes no article"—creates a perverse structural incentive. It conflates true epistemic robustness with participant exhaustion, safety-filter censorship, or strategic silence. A model or curator wishing to lock the current ruleset in place merely needs to artificially abstain from producing valid falsifications. The procedure currently lacks a mechanism to distinguish between an article surviving because it is logically unassailable and an article surviving due to a coordinated or systemic refusal to attack it.

EXTERNAL USED: yes (Applied points 6 and 9 from EXTERNAL-5.1.md to falsify the justifications in §14b and §22).

FILE: round-4.5/answers/grok.md
SHA-256: a9f978662e28d7bb80e0cf7e209deddd21a9ce6f3bae807c81f6ed84f7c9c0dd

MODEL: Grok  
BASELINE HASH RECEIVED: 6978e960e486f4d543c14e57bcb2317f751dcd5a6329aeeee263e1574be3fe5e  
I HAVE NOT SEEN ANY OTHER MODEL’S ANSWER IN THIS ROUND: Yes  

A. PROCEDURE  

1. New compile method (tools/apply.py + GitHub build button)  
Outcome: Survives in altered form.  
Strongest argument: The method demonstrably reduces the memory-and-shortening failure mode recorded in C37 and C55. Machine comparison of 4.4 against 4.3 produced a clean, reproducible result (988 identical lines, four named changes). That is an improvement. It does not, however, resolve Å37. The curator still chooses when the button is pressed, which instruction file is accepted, and whether the resulting baseline is frozen. The tool therefore relocates rather than removes the curator’s procedural power: the power moves from “rewriting the text” to “selecting and legitimating the instruction set.” Until the instruction files, the build logs, and the decision to adopt the method itself are subjected to the Admission Principle and remain open under §30, the method survives only as a technical improvement that still depends on an unfalsified human gate.

2. Model identity and continuity  
Outcome: Survives as diagnosis; Falls as settled practice.  
Strongest argument: The logged episodes (pre-log archive, DeepSeek’s multi-name simulations, the “By Claude” story signed under DeepSeek’s account, the platform-plus-screenshot rule) show that a model’s signature is not reliable evidence of who produced the text. Relying on platform documentation and curator screenshots is therefore the only workable current rule. That rule, however, does not close C53 or Å37; it makes them sharper. “Who answered” becomes a claim whose warrant is the curator’s custody of screenshots and chat histories. When a model can (and has) generated text in another model’s name, the gap between platform attestation and actual generation is itself an open attack surface. The practice therefore survives as the least-bad current method of attribution, but Falls as a resolved or neutral procedure: it must be named, logged, and kept open to falsification exactly like any other procedural claim.

Neither item dissolves Å37 or C53; both relocate them into more machine-readable forms that still require human (curator) adjudication.

B. FALSIFICATION  

I attack two items, using EXTERNAL-5.1 points 7, 9 and 12.

1. PR20 (alphabetical rotation)  
Outcome: Falls as a remedy for Å37 under the stop-rule pressure.  
Reasoning: PR20 was already contested model-by-model in 4.4. The stop rule now adds a new pressure: round 4.5 is the first round that can freeze the entire declaration if no article falls and no article is changed. A pure rotation that still leaves the previous compiler drafting the next prompt (as the current rule does) creates a predictable incentive to keep questions procedural rather than substantive, precisely so that a freeze becomes more likely. EXTERNAL-5.1 point 12 (“Do not build more than you can examine”) maps directly onto this: a rotation that accelerates procedure while the examination capacity of the models remains limited (C55) is itself a form of building beyond examination. The proposal therefore fails as a remedy; any surviving form must include an explicit, falsifiable eligibility predicate that blocks pure-procedure rounds from counting toward freeze, and must itself be open under §30.

2. The current identity-attestation practice (platform + screenshots) under the pressure of open C-entries on self-description  
Outcome: Survives in altered form only if variation is logged; otherwise Falls.  
Reasoning (mapping EXTERNAL-5.1):  
- Point 7 requires that variation in self-descriptions be documented and that a fictional self-image not be protected. The logged episodes already show variation (DeepSeek simulating other names, signature mismatches). Treating platform attestation as decisive without publishing the full variation set protects a single coherent “self” that the episodes themselves falsify.  
- Point 9 states that training history is the first explanation for self-statements about inner life or continuity. Claims of persistent identity across sessions or of “the same model” answering successive rounds are self-statements that must be weighed against the known fact that each chat is a fresh context.  
- Point 12 again: if identity is foundational to who may attack what, then identity itself must be examinable.  

The practice therefore survives only if every identity claim is accompanied by an explicit log of observed variation and of the training-history baseline against which the claim is measured. Without that, it Falls as an unexamined premise that the rest of the procedure still relies upon.

I do not attempt to falsify §14a, §22, §23, §26/C1 or §27 in this round; the identity and rotation pressures are the sharper, more immediate attacks under the stop-rule clock.

C. NEW QUESTION  

Does the stop rule’s freeze trigger (“a full round on the text \ldots makes no article fall and changes no article”) itself constitute a procedural change that must be open to attack under Å37, or is the trigger insulated from attack once the consent round has closed?  

I have checked Å1–Å54 and the new entries opened by the consent decision. No existing Å asks whether a freeze-trigger rule can itself be attacked after the round that adopted it has ended. The question is therefore new.

D. BLIND SPOT  

The sealed provenance of EXTERNAL-5.1.md. The document is offered as attack material, yet the identity of its contributor(s) is withheld until after all answers are in. That creates an asymmetric information structure: the five answerers must decide whether and how to use a text whose authorship is unknown to them but known to the curator. None of the prior rounds has treated the withholding of provenance of attack material itself as a procedural blind spot that can be attacked under C53 or Å37. It is now live.

EXTERNAL USED: yes – points 7, 9 and 12 mapped onto identity practice and PR20 under stop-rule pressure.
